I end up staying home. \n\nIt's a shame, really, because the theater is doing The Music Man this season. Such a classic. My niece, Emily, she played the mayor's wife in her high school production years ago. She wore this hideous feathered hat that kept hitting the boy next to her right in the face during the big musical numbers. We laughed so hard from the front row... \n\nBut yes, I've missed my volunteer shifts there. And, oh, it's even affecting my job. I've actually been late turning in some of my bookkeeping work recently because I get so distracted and upset worrying about my arm. Which is so unlike me! I pride myself on being prompt. Though, to be fair, one of our clients—he owns a local plumbing business—he brings me his monthly expenses in a literal paper grocery bag. Crinkled up gas station receipts and coffee cups and everything. I told him, I said, 'Frank, they make smartphone apps for this now,' but you know how stubborn men his age can be about technology... \n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, just that I've been falling behind a bit because of all this.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the scar on your wrist, do you find yourself frequently checking it in the mirror, comparing it to how you think it should look, or feeling like it looks much worse than it actually does?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I do. Before I leave the house, I always catch myself stopping by the hall mirror. I'll check my wrist a few times, you know, just to see how it catches the light. And then I'll try pulling my sleeve down, or maybe I'll put on that chunky silver bracelet my husband bought me for our anniversary. Our thirtieth anniversary, actually. We went to this lovely bed and breakfast up in the mountains, though the drive up there was absolutely terrifying. The roads were so winding, and you know how people drive these days, just flying around those blind curves like they're in the Indianapolis 500. My husband, bless him, he drove the whole way while I just gripped the door handle and prayed. \n\nBut anyway, I'll put on the bracelet or adjust my sleeve, and sometimes I'll even ask him, I'll say, 'Does this look obvious?' And he always tells me it's barely there, that nobody is going to notice. He's very sweet about it. Though he's terrible at noticing things in general. Last week I got my hair trimmed—just a couple of inches off the bottom, nothing drastic—and he didn't say a word for three days. Three days! I finally had to ask him if he noticed anything different, and he guessed I bought new glasses. Men, honestly. But yes, I do check it in the mirror a few times before I go out.\n\nDoctor: When you look at the scar, do you feel like it looks abnormal or disfigured in a way that other people would also notice and be disturbed by, even if they don’t mention it?\n\nPatient: Disfigured? Oh heavens, no. I wouldn't say it's disturbing to look at, like a horror movie or anything. I don't think people are gasping in shock when they see it. \n\nSpeaking of a shock, though, you should see the state of the sidewalks in my neighborhood. The oak tree roots are pushing up the concrete absolutely everywhere. I nearly tripped last Tuesday walking over to Susan's house to drop off a casserole. She had a little knee surgery—nothing major, just a quick scope—but I thought I'd bring her my chicken divan. It has a pinch of curry powder in it, which is my secret ingredient. Most people just use mayonnaise and canned cream of mushroom soup, but the curry really gives it a nice warmth. My mother taught me that trick. \n\nAnyway, the sidewalks are an absolute hazard, and the homeowner's association refuses to do anything about it. I look at their annual budget reports, being a numbers person, and I know for a fact they have the reserve funds to fix it. They just choose to spend it on replanting the front entrance flowers every three months. It's completely frivolous spending when there are real safety issues to address... \n\nWait, I'm sorry, what was the question? Oh, right, my wrist. No, I don't think it's horrifying or disturbing to other people. It's just a scar. It just bothers me that their eyes might be drawn to it, you know?\n\nDoctor: Is your worry about the scar mainly about a specific feature, like its size, shape, or color, or is it more that you're concerned people will judge you negatively for having a scar at all?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not really about the size or the color or anything specific like that. I don't think it's ugly, per se. It's just... I worry about what people might think if they see it. You know, people can be so judgmental. They see a mark on your wrist and suddenly they're making all sorts of assumptions about your life or your mental state. \n\nIt reminds me of this woman in my bridge club, Martha. She showed up one week with this tiny little bruise on her cheek—she said she bumped into a cabinet door in the kitchen, which, honestly, we've all done. I once nearly knocked myself out opening the freezer door too fast when I was trying to grab a bag of frozen peas for my husband's sprained ankle. He twisted it playing pickleball of all things. I keep telling him, 'Arthur, you are sixty-five years old, you do not need to be diving for a plastic ball,' but he insists it's good cardio. Anyway, Martha had this little bruise, and I swear, the entire afternoon, the other ladies were whispering behind their cards, wondering if her husband was drinking again. Which is a terrible thing to gossip about, just terrible. People just love to invent stories when they see something out of the ordinary. \n\nSo, yes, I suppose that's what it is. I just don't want anyone jumping to conclusions or whispering about me the way they did about Martha. I just want to blend in and not give anyone a reason to stare.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry more about people noticing the scar because you think they’ll judge you for having it, or because you think it looks unattractive to them?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't really think it's about them finding it unattractive. I mean, I know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me in the mirror. But when I'm out, like at church on Sundays or at my bridge club, I just keep feeling like everyone's eyes go right to it. It's like it's the very first thing they notice about me now, instead of my outfit or my hair. \n\nSpeaking of hair, I finally got an appointment with that new stylist downtown, the one everyone is raving about. My usual girl, Brenda, she's lovely, really she is, but she's been so distracted lately. Her daughter just started college out of state—somewhere in Ohio, I think? Or maybe Indiana. One of those states that's mostly cornfields. Anyway, Brenda's been a nervous wreck about it, constantly checking her phone during my color processing. Last time she left the toner on for an extra ten minutes and I swear my highlights looked almost purple in the sunlight. I had to wash it with clarifying shampoo three times before it looked normal again. Clarifying shampoo is so stripping, you know, you really have to follow it up with a deep conditioner or your ends will just snap right off. \n\nBut yes, at church or bridge, I just feel like they're all staring right at my wrist, wondering about it.\n\nDoctor: When you're worried about people noticing your scar, is your main concern that they might think badly of you or judge you for having it, or is it more about how it looks to you?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... I mean, I don't know if it's strictly one or the other. I just don't like it. But mostly, yes, I think I just worry they're going to think badly of me. Like I'm careless or, I don't know, unstable or something. \n\nYou know, people are just so quick to judge these days. It’s like when they put those new self-checkout lanes in at the grocery store. I was trying to scan a bag of honeycrisp apples—they’re my absolute favorite for baking, they hold their shape so much better than a McIntosh, which just turns to mush in a pie. My grandmother used to make the most wonderful apple pie, she’d put a slice of sharp cheddar cheese right on top of the crust while it was still warm. Sounds odd, I know, but the salty and sweet combination is just divine. Anyway, I was trying to scan these apples, and the machine kept yelling 'unexpected item in the bagging area.' I wasn't doing anything wrong, but the woman behind me in line let out this loud, dramatic sigh and rolled her eyes, like I was doing it on purpose just to ruin her day. People have no patience anymore, none at all. They just assume the worst about you in an instant.\n\nSo, yes, I suppose that’s what bothers me. I just don’t want them looking at my wrist and making up some terrible story in their heads about who I am.\n\nDoctor: When you're at home alone and not expecting to see anyone, do you still find yourself preoccupied with how the scar looks, or is your concern mainly when you're around other people?\n\nPatient: When I'm home alone? Well, I suppose I don't think about it quite as much. I mean, like I was saying earlier, I know the scar is probably not as bad as it actually looks to me. It’s really just when I’m out—at church or playing bridge—that I get this awful feeling that everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the absolute first thing they notice about me now. \n\nBut at home, well, I'm usually just too busy to dwell on it. I still do some part-time bookkeeping for my nephew's landscaping business. He started it three years ago, and I had to sit him down and say, \"David, you cannot just keep your business receipts in a Nike shoebox.\" Young people think the IRS doesn't care about small, local businesses, but let me tell you, they will absolutely audit you over a single missing invoice for mulch. I set him up on this wonderful accounting software, though my desktop computer has been acting up lately. It makes this dreadful whirring noise whenever I open a spreadsheet. Arthur insists it's just the cooling fan, but I am convinced it's a virus from when he was looking up vintage car parts online. He loves those old 1960s Mustangs, but the websites he goes to look so incredibly shady, with all those pop-up advertisements for things I won't even mention. \n\nAnyway... what was I saying? Oh, right, being at home. Yes, when it's just me in the house, or just me and Arthur, I don't fret over it the same way. It's really only when I have to face other people that it consumes my thoughts.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the scar, do you feel that it looks physically wrong or flawed to you in a way that feels unbearable, even if others say it's barely noticeable?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say it's unbearable to look at. I mean, like I said, I know it's just a regular scar. It’s not like it’s some hideous, mangled thing. Arthur always tells me it's barely noticeable anyway. \n\nBut you know, Arthur says that about absolutely everything. Just last month, we had this awful brown water stain on the living room ceiling from all that heavy rain we got in April. I pointed it out to him, and he said, \"Oh, it's barely noticeable, just angle the floor lamp away from it.\" A lamp! As if changing the lighting is going to fix a leaky roof. We ended up having to call a roofer, this young man named Tommy. Though his estimate was just highway robbery, honestly. I know building materials have gotten terribly expensive lately, but four hundred dollars just to patch a few asphalt shingles? And then he left his extension ladder right in the middle of our driveway for three whole days. I had to park my Buick out on the street, and they do street sweeping on Thursdays, so I was just terrified I was going to get a fifty-dollar parking ticket all because Tommy couldn't be bothered to pack up his truck properly. \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, the scar. Right. No, I don't sit there looking at it thinking it's physically unbearable or completely wrong. I know what it is. I just don't like it, and I really just don't want other people staring at it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that worrying about the way your scar looks takes up a lot of your time or causes you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say it takes up a lot of my time, no. I mean, it's distressing when I'm out, like I mentioned, but I don't sit around all day agonizing over it. I just try to keep busy. \n\nKeeping busy is really the best medicine, isn't it? My neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, she retired last year from the post office, and she just sits in her front window all day watching the street. She called the police on the Amazon delivery driver last week because he parked a little too close to her hydrangeas. Can you imagine? The poor man was just trying to drop off a package. I order from Amazon quite a bit now, mostly for Arthur's supplements. He read in one of those magazines at the barber shop that taking turmeric is good for your joints, but I swear the capsules smell like old soup. I tried making a curry once back in the seventies, when everyone was trying all those international recipes, but Arthur wouldn't touch it. He's strictly a meat and potatoes man. \n\nBut, oh, I'm sorry, I'm wandering again. No, it doesn't take up all my time. I just get anxious when I have to go out and face people, but it's not like it ruins my whole day.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your concern about the scar is mainly about how it makes you feel about your appearance, or is it more about what people might think of you because of it?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it's really about what other people think. Like I said, I know it's just a regular scar, but I just can't stand the thought of people staring at it and judging me. It's the judgment, really. \n\nPeople can be so terribly judgmental these days. Have you noticed that? Just last Sunday at church, Mary Beth Lawson was whispering to her sister the entire time Reverend Thomas was giving his sermon. The entire time! And she was wearing this hat, oh, you wouldn't believe it. It looked like a very large, very angry bird had nested right on top of her head. I don't know where she buys her church clothes, but she always looks like she's going to a garden party in 1985. I used to make my own dresses, you know. I had a beautiful Singer sewing machine that my mother gave me when Arthur and I first got married. I made all my own curtains, too. But the tension on the bobbin got all messed up a few years ago, and trying to find someone who actually repairs sewing machines anymore is like trying to find a needle in a haystack. Everyone just throws things away and buys new ones now. It's a real shame, the amount of waste. \n\nBut, oh, I'm sorry, what were you asking? Oh, right, the scar. Yes, it's definitely about what other people think. I just don't want to be the subject of their whispering, you know?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Well, I suppose it's really about what other people think. Like I said, I know it's just a regular scar, but I just can't stand the thought of people staring at it and judging me. It's the judgment, really. 

People can be so terribly judgmental these days. Have you noticed that? Just last Sunday at church, Mary Beth Lawson was whispering to her sister the entire time Reverend Thomas was giving his sermon. The entire time! And she was wearing this hat, oh, you wouldn't believe it. It looked like a very large, very angry bird had nested right on top of her head. I don't know where she buys her church clothes, but she always looks like she's going to a garden party in 1985. I used to make my own dresses, you know. I had a beautiful Singer sewing machine that my mother gave me when Arthur and I first got married. I made all my own curtains, too. But the tension on the bobbin got all messed up a few years ago, and trying to find someone who actually repairs sewing machines anymore is like trying to find a needle in a haystack. Everyone just throws things away and buys new ones now. It's a real shame, the amount of waste. 

But, oh, I'm sorry, what were you asking? Oh, right, the scar. Yes, it's definitely about what other people think. I just don't want to be the subject of their whispering, you know?

He loves those old 1960s Mustangs, but the websites he goes to look so incredibly shady, with all those pop-up advertisements for things I won't even mention. \n\nAnyway... what was I saying? Oh, right, being at home. Yes, when it's just me in the house, or just me and Arthur, I don't fret over it the same way. It's really only when I have to face other people that it consumes my thoughts.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the scar, do you feel that it looks physically wrong or flawed to you in a way that feels unbearable, even if others say it's barely noticeable?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say it's unbearable to look at. I mean, like I said, I know it's just a regular scar. It’s not like it’s some hideous, mangled thing. Arthur always tells me it's barely noticeable anyway. \n\nBut you know, Arthur says that about absolutely everything. Just last month, we had this awful brown water stain on the living room ceiling from all that heavy rain we got in April. I pointed it out to him, and he said, \"Oh, it's barely noticeable, just angle the floor lamp away from it.\" A lamp! As if changing the lighting is going to fix a leaky roof. We ended up having to call a roofer, this young man named Tommy. Though his estimate was just highway robbery, honestly. I know building materials have gotten terribly expensive lately, but four hundred dollars just to patch a few asphalt shingles? And then he left his extension ladder right in the middle of our driveway for three whole days. I had to park my Buick out on the street, and they do street sweeping on Thursdays, so I was just terrified I was going to get a fifty-dollar parking ticket all because Tommy couldn't be bothered to pack up his truck properly. \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, the scar. Right. No, I don't sit there looking at it thinking it's physically unbearable or completely wrong. I know what it is. I just don't like it, and I really just don't want other people staring at it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that worrying about the way your scar looks takes up a lot of your time or causes you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say it takes up a lot of my time, no. I mean, it's distressing when I'm out, like I mentioned, but I don't sit around all day agonizing over it. I just try to keep busy. \n\nKeeping busy is really the best medicine, isn't it? My neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, she retired last year from the post office, and she just sits in her front window all day watching the street. She called the police on the Amazon delivery driver last week because he parked a little too close to her hydrangeas. Can you imagine? The poor man was just trying to drop off a package. I order from Amazon quite a bit now, mostly for Arthur's supplements. He read in one of those magazines at the barber shop that taking turmeric is good for your joints, but I swear the capsules smell like old soup. I tried making a curry once back in the seventies, when everyone was trying all those international recipes, but Arthur wouldn't touch it. He's strictly a meat and potatoes man. \n\nBut, oh, I'm sorry, I'm wandering again. No, it doesn't take up all my time. I just get anxious when I have to go out and face people, but it's not like it ruins my whole day.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your concern about the scar is mainly about how it makes you feel about your appearance, or is it more about what people might think of you because of it?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it's really about what other people think. Everyone just throws things away and buys new ones now. It's a real shame, the amount of waste. \n\nBut, oh, I'm sorry, what were you asking? Oh, right, the scar. Well, I know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. Arthur tells me he can barely even see it most days. But when I’m at church or playing bridge, I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the very first thing they notice about me now. 

Especially at bridge, you know, because your hands are right out there on the table holding the cards. We use those large-print playing cards now because Helen's eyesight is getting so poor. She absolutely refuses to get cataract surgery, which is just silly to me. My cousin Barbara had it done last year, and she said it was an absolute breeze. In and out in an afternoon, and now she doesn't even need her reading glasses for the Sunday crossword. I still need mine, unfortunately, especially when I'm doing the bookkeeping for the church. The numbers on the ledgers just seem to blur together if the lighting isn't exactly right. We've been trying to get the fluorescent bulbs replaced in the church office for three months, but the maintenance man, Gary, is moving at a snail's pace. He claims the specific bulbs are on backorder, but I saw him at the hardware store last Tuesday just buying bags of birdseed. He's very involved with the local Audubon society, which is nice, I suppose, but it doesn't help me balance the church budget in the dark. 

Oh, dear, I'm wandering off again, aren't I? I apologize. But yes, even if people say it's barely noticeable, I just sit there holding my cards feeling like my wrist is the only thing anyone is looking at.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.22, F40.11\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it's... it's this scar on my wrist. I know, deep down, it's probably not as bad as it looks to me. But when I’m at church, or when I play bridge... I play bridge every Thursday with Martha and Susan. Martha actually just had her whole kitchen redone. Beautiful cherry wood cabinets, though I told her the quote her contractor gave her was absolute highway robbery. I'm a bookkeeper, you see, so I always look at the numbers. I do the ledgers for a little auditing firm downtown, have for years since I took some college courses in it. \n\nBut anyway, where was I? Oh, right, the scar. When I'm sitting there at the card table with the ladies, or in the pews on Sunday, I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it. Like it’s the very first thing they notice about me now.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid going to church, playing bridge, or doing other activities because you're worried about people noticing the scar on your wrist?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't quit bridge, but... I have been skipping some of my other things. Like the church choir, and my volunteer shifts at the community theater. I just get so caught up in my bedroom, trying on different long-sleeved blouses or arranging my watch and bracelets to make sure my wrist won't be noticed, that I just... I end up staying home. \n\nIt's a shame, really, because the theater is doing The Music Man this season. Such a classic. My niece, Emily, she played the mayor's wife in her high school production years ago. She wore this hideous feathered hat that kept hitting the boy next to her right in the face during the big musical numbers. We laughed so hard from the front row... \n\nBut yes, I've missed my volunteer shifts there. And, oh, it's even affecting my job. I've actually been late turning in some of my bookkeeping work recently because I get so distracted and upset worrying about my arm. Which is so unlike me! I pride myself on being prompt. Though, to be fair, one of our clients—he owns a local plumbing business—he brings me his monthly expenses in a literal paper grocery bag. Crinkled up gas station receipts and coffee cups and everything. I told him, I said, 'Frank, they make smartphone apps for this now,' but you know how stubborn men his age can be about technology... \n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, just that I've been falling behind a bit because of all this.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the scar on your wrist, do you find yourself frequently checking it in the mirror, comparing it to how you think it should look, or feeling like it looks much worse than it actually does?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I do. Before I leave the house, I always catch myself stopping by the hall mirror. I'll check my wrist a few times, you know, just to see how it catches the light. And then I'll try pulling my sleeve down, or maybe I'll put on that chunky silver bracelet my husband bought me for our anniversary. Our thirtieth anniversary, actually. We went to this lovely bed and breakfast up in the mountains, though the drive up there was absolutely terrifying. The roads were so winding, and you know how people drive these days, just flying around those blind curves like they're in the Indianapolis 500. My husband, bless him, he drove the whole way while I just gripped the door handle and prayed. \n\nBut anyway, I'll put on the bracelet or adjust my sleeve, and sometimes I'll even ask him, I'll say, 'Does this look obvious?' And he always tells me it's barely there, that nobody is going to notice. He's very sweet about it. Though he's terrible at noticing things in general. Last week I got my hair trimmed—just a couple of inches off the bottom, nothing drastic—and he didn't say a word for three days. Three days! I finally had to ask him if he noticed anything different, and he guessed I bought new glasses. Men, honestly. But yes, I do check it in the mirror a few times before I go out.\n\nDoctor: When you look at the scar, do you feel like it looks abnormal or disfigured in a way that other people would also notice and be disturbed by, even if they don’t mention it?\n\nPatient: Disfigured? Oh heavens, no. I wouldn't say it's disturbing to look at, like a horror movie or anything. I don't think people are gasping in shock when they see it. \n\nSpeaking of a shock, though, you should see the state of the sidewalks in my neighborhood. The oak tree roots are pushing up the concrete absolutely everywhere. I nearly tripped last Tuesday walking over to Susan's house to drop off a casserole. She had a little knee surgery—nothing major, just a quick scope—but I thought I'd bring her my chicken divan. It has a pinch of curry powder in it, which is my secret ingredient. Most people just use mayonnaise and canned cream of mushroom soup, but the curry really gives it a nice warmth. My mother taught me that trick. \n\nAnyway, the sidewalks are an absolute hazard, and the homeowner's association refuses to do anything about it. I look at their annual budget reports, being a numbers person, and I know for a fact they have the reserve funds to fix it. They just choose to spend it on replanting the front entrance flowers every three months. It's completely frivolous spending when there are real safety issues to address... \n\nWait, I'm sorry, what was the question? Oh, right, my wrist. No, I don't think it's horrifying or disturbing to other people. It's just a scar. It just bothers me that their eyes might be drawn to it, you know?\n\nDoctor: Is your worry about the scar mainly about a specific feature, like its size, shape, or color, or is it more that you're concerned people will judge you negatively for having a scar at all?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not really about the size or the color or anything specific like that. I don't think it's ugly, per se. It's just... I worry about what people might think if they see it. You know, people can be so judgmental. They see a mark on your wrist and suddenly they're making all sorts of assumptions about your life or your mental state. \n\nIt reminds me of this woman in my bridge club, Martha. She showed up one week with this tiny little bruise on her cheek—she said she bumped into a cabinet door in the kitchen, which, honestly, we've all done. I once nearly knocked myself out opening the freezer door too fast when I was trying to grab a bag of frozen peas for my husband's sprained ankle. He twisted it playing pickleball of all things. I keep telling him, 'Arthur, you are sixty-five years old, you do not need to be diving for a plastic ball,' but he insists it's good cardio. Anyway, Martha had this little bruise, and I swear, the entire afternoon, the other ladies were whispering behind their cards, wondering if her husband was drinking again. Which is a terrible thing to gossip about, just terrible. People just love to invent stories when they see something out of the ordinary. \n\nSo, yes, I suppose that's what it is. I just don't want anyone jumping to conclusions or whispering about me the way they did about Martha. I just want to blend in and not give anyone a reason to stare.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry more about people noticing the scar because you think they’ll judge you for having it, or because you think it looks unattractive to them?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't really think it's about them finding it unattractive. I mean, I know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me in the mirror. But when I'm out, like at church on Sundays or at my bridge club, I just keep feeling like everyone's eyes go right to it. It's like it's the very first thing they notice about me now, instead of my outfit or my hair. \n\nSpeaking of hair, I finally got an appointment with that new stylist downtown, the one everyone is raving about. My usual girl, Brenda, she's lovely, really she is, but she's been so distracted lately. Her daughter just started college out of state—somewhere in Ohio, I think? Or maybe Indiana. One of those states that's mostly cornfields. Anyway, Brenda's been a nervous wreck about it, constantly checking her phone during my color processing. Last time she left the toner on for an extra ten minutes and I swear my highlights looked almost purple in the sunlight. I had to wash it with clarifying shampoo three times before it looked normal again. Clarifying shampoo is so stripping, you know, you really have to follow it up with a deep conditioner or your ends will just snap right off. \n\nBut yes, at church or bridge, I just feel like they're all staring right at my wrist, wondering about it.\n\nDoctor: When you're worried about people noticing your scar, is your main concern that they might think badly of you or judge you for having it, or is it more about how it looks to you?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... I mean, I don't know if it's strictly one or the other. I just don't like it. But mostly, yes, I think I just worry they're going to think badly of me. Like I'm careless or, I don't know, unstable or something. \n\nYou know, people are just so quick to judge these days. It’s like when they put those new self-checkout lanes in at the grocery store. I was trying to scan a bag of honeycrisp apples—they’re my absolute favorite for baking, they hold their shape so much better than a McIntosh, which just turns to mush in a pie. My grandmother used to make the most wonderful apple pie, she’d put a slice of sharp cheddar cheese right on top of the crust while it was still warm. Sounds odd, I know, but the salty and sweet combination is just divine. Anyway, I was trying to scan these apples, and the machine kept yelling 'unexpected item in the bagging area.' I wasn't doing anything wrong, but the woman behind me in line let out this loud, dramatic sigh and rolled her eyes, like I was doing it on purpose just to ruin her day. People have no patience anymore, none at all. They just assume the worst about you in an instant.\n\nSo, yes, I suppose that’s what bothers me. I just don’t want them looking at my wrist and making up some terrible story in their heads about who I am.\n\nDoctor: When you're at home alone and not expecting to see anyone, do you still find yourself preoccupied with how the scar looks, or is your concern mainly when you're around other people?\n\nPatient: When I'm home alone? Well, I suppose I don't think about it quite as much. I mean, like I was saying earlier, I know the scar is probably not as bad as it actually looks to me. It’s really just when I’m out—at church or playing bridge—that I get this awful feeling that everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the absolute first thing they notice about me now. \n\nBut at home, well, I'm usually just too busy to dwell on it. I still do some part-time bookkeeping for my nephew's landscaping business. He started it three years ago, and I had to sit him down and say, \"David, you cannot just keep your business receipts in a Nike shoebox.\" Young people think the IRS doesn't care about small, local businesses, but let me tell you, they will absolutely audit you over a single missing invoice for mulch. I set him up on this wonderful accounting software, though my desktop computer has been acting up lately. It makes this dreadful whirring noise whenever I open a spreadsheet. Arthur insists it's just the cooling fan, but I am convinced it's a virus from when he was looking up vintage car parts online. He loves those old 1960s Mustangs, but the websites he goes to look so incredibly shady, with all those pop-up advertisements for things I won't even mention. \n\nAnyway... what was I saying? Oh, right, being at home. Yes, when it's just me in the house, or just me and Arthur, I don't fret over it the same way. It's really only when I have to face other people that it consumes my thoughts.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the scar, do you feel that it looks physically wrong or flawed to you in a way that feels unbearable, even if others say it's barely noticeable?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say it's unbearable to look at. I mean, like I said, I know it's just a regular scar. It’s not like it’s some hideous, mangled thing. Arthur always tells me it's barely noticeable anyway. \n\nBut you know, Arthur says that about absolutely everything. Just last month, we had this awful brown water stain on the living room ceiling from all that heavy rain we got in April. I pointed it out to him, and he said, \"Oh, it's barely noticeable, just angle the floor lamp away from it.\" A lamp! As if changing the lighting is going to fix a leaky roof. We ended up having to call a roofer, this young man named Tommy. Though his estimate was just highway robbery, honestly. I know building materials have gotten terribly expensive lately, but four hundred dollars just to patch a few asphalt shingles? And then he left his extension ladder right in the middle of our driveway for three whole days. I had to park my Buick out on the street, and they do street sweeping on Thursdays, so I was just terrified I was going to get a fifty-dollar parking ticket all because Tommy couldn't be bothered to pack up his truck properly. \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, the scar. Right. No, I don't sit there looking at it thinking it's physically unbearable or completely wrong. I know what it is. I just don't like it, and I really just don't want other people staring at it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that worrying about the way your scar looks takes up a lot of your time or causes you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say it takes up a lot of my time, no. I mean, it's distressing when I'm out, like I mentioned, but I don't sit around all day agonizing over it. I just try to keep busy. \n\nKeeping busy is really the best medicine, isn't it? My neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, she retired last year from the post office, and she just sits in her front window all day watching the street. She called the police on the Amazon delivery driver last week because he parked a little too close to her hydrangeas. Can you imagine? The poor man was just trying to drop off a package. I order from Amazon quite a bit now, mostly for Arthur's supplements. He read in one of those magazines at the barber shop that taking turmeric is good for your joints, but I swear the capsules smell like old soup. I tried making a curry once back in the seventies, when everyone was trying all those international recipes, but Arthur wouldn't touch it. He's strictly a meat and potatoes man. \n\nBut, oh, I'm sorry, I'm wandering again. No, it doesn't take up all my time. I just get anxious when I have to go out and face people, but it's not like it ruins my whole day.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your concern about the scar is mainly about how it makes you feel about your appearance, or is it more about what people might think of you because of it?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it's really about what other people think. It's the judgment, really. \n\nPeople can be so terribly judgmental these days. Have you noticed that? Just last Sunday at church, Mary Beth Lawson was whispering to her sister the entire time Reverend Thomas was giving his sermon. The entire time! And she was wearing this hat, oh, you wouldn't believe it. It looked like a very large, very angry bird had nested right on top of her head. I don't know where she buys her church clothes, but she always looks like she's going to a garden party in 1985. I used to make my own dresses, you know. Well, it's really the people. I mean, when I look at it in the mirror at home, I know it's just a little line. But the moment I step out the door, it just feels like this glaring mistake on my arm that everyone is staring at. 

It's just like when I spilled that tiny drop of coffee on my white blouse before the church bake sale last month. I scrubbed and scrubbed with club soda—my mother always swore by club soda for stains, she used to buy it by the case at the Piggly Wiggly. Do you remember Piggly Wiggly? They had the best produce section, always so crisp. The manager, Mr. Henderson, would always save the best peaches for my mother when they were in season. We used to make peach cobbler every Sunday in July. The secret is a pinch of nutmeg in the crust, not just cinnamon. But anyway, I scrubbed that spot, and Arthur said he couldn't see a thing, but I spent the entire bake sale with my arms crossed over my chest, just certain that everyone was whispering about the church bookkeeper looking unkempt. I ended up leaving early and missing the silent auction entirely, which was a shame because they had a beautiful handmade quilt I wanted to bid on. 

But yes, to answer your question, it's definitely the worry about what other people are thinking when they look at me.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.22, F40.11\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it's... it's this scar on my wrist. I know, deep down, it's probably not as bad as it looks to me. But when I’m at church, or when I play bridge... I play bridge every Thursday with Martha and Susan. Martha actually just had her whole kitchen redone. Beautiful cherry wood cabinets, though I told her the quote her contractor gave her was absolute highway robbery. I'm a bookkeeper, you see, so I always look at the numbers. I do the ledgers for a little auditing firm downtown, have for years since I took some college courses in it. \n\nBut anyway, where was I? Oh, right, the scar. When I'm sitting there at the card table with the ladies, or in the pews on Sunday, I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it. Like it’s the very first thing they notice about me now.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid going to church, playing bridge, or doing other activities because you're worried about people noticing the scar on your wrist?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't quit bridge, but... I have been skipping some of my other things. Like the church choir, and my volunteer shifts at the community theater. I just get so caught up in my bedroom, trying on different long-sleeved blouses or arranging my watch and bracelets to make sure my wrist won't be noticed, that I just... I end up staying home. \n\nIt's a shame, really, because the theater is doing The Music Man this season. Such a classic. My niece, Emily, she played the mayor's wife in her high school production years ago. She wore this hideous feathered hat that kept hitting the boy next to her right in the face during the big musical numbers. We laughed so hard from the front row... \n\nBut yes, I've missed my volunteer shifts there. And, oh, it's even affecting my job. I've actually been late turning in some of my bookkeeping work recently because I get so distracted and upset worrying about my arm. Which is so unlike me! I pride myself on being prompt. Though, to be fair, one of our clients—he owns a local plumbing business—he brings me his monthly expenses in a literal paper grocery bag. Crinkled up gas station receipts and coffee cups and everything. I told him, I said, 'Frank, they make smartphone apps for this now,' but you know how stubborn men his age can be about technology... \n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, just that I've been falling behind a bit because of all this.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the scar on your wrist, do you find yourself frequently checking it in the mirror, comparing it to how you think it should look, or feeling like it looks much worse than it actually does?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I do. Before I leave the house, I always catch myself stopping by the hall mirror. I'll check my wrist a few times, you know, just to see how it catches the light. And then I'll try pulling my sleeve down, or maybe I'll put on that chunky silver bracelet my husband bought me for our anniversary. Our thirtieth anniversary, actually. We went to this lovely bed and breakfast up in the mountains, though the drive up there was absolutely terrifying. The roads were so winding, and you know how people drive these days, just flying around those blind curves like they're in the Indianapolis 500. My husband, bless him, he drove the whole way while I just gripped the door handle and prayed. \n\nBut anyway, I'll put on the bracelet or adjust my sleeve, and sometimes I'll even ask him, I'll say, 'Does this look obvious?' And he always tells me it's barely there, that nobody is going to notice. He's very sweet about it. Though he's terrible at noticing things in general. Last week I got my hair trimmed—just a couple of inches off the bottom, nothing drastic—and he didn't say a word for three days. Three days! I finally had to ask him if he noticed anything different, and he guessed I bought new glasses. Men, honestly. But yes, I do check it in the mirror a few times before I go out.\n\nDoctor: When you look at the scar, do you feel like it looks abnormal or disfigured in a way that other people would also notice and be disturbed by, even if they don’t mention it?\n\nPatient: Disfigured? Oh heavens, no. I wouldn't say it's disturbing to look at, like a horror movie or anything. I don't think people are gasping in shock when they see it. \n\nSpeaking of a shock, though, you should see the state of the sidewalks in my neighborhood. The oak tree roots are pushing up the concrete absolutely everywhere. I nearly tripped last Tuesday walking over to Susan's house to drop off a casserole. She had a little knee surgery—nothing major, just a quick scope—but I thought I'd bring her my chicken divan. It has a pinch of curry powder in it, which is my secret ingredient. Most people just use mayonnaise and canned cream of mushroom soup, but the curry really gives it a nice warmth. My mother taught me that trick. \n\nAnyway, the sidewalks are an absolute hazard, and the homeowner's association refuses to do anything about it. I look at their annual budget reports, being a numbers person, and I know for a fact they have the reserve funds to fix it. They just choose to spend it on replanting the front entrance flowers every three months. It's completely frivolous spending when there are real safety issues to address... \n\nWait, I'm sorry, what was the question? Oh, right, my wrist. No, I don't think it's horrifying or disturbing to other people. It's just a scar. It just bothers me that their eyes might be drawn to it, you know?\n\nDoctor: Is your worry about the scar mainly about a specific feature, like its size, shape, or color, or is it more that you're concerned people will judge you negatively for having a scar at all?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not really about the size or the color or anything specific like that. I don't think it's ugly, per se. It's just... I worry about what people might think if they see it. You know, people can be so judgmental. They see a mark on your wrist and suddenly they're making all sorts of assumptions about your life or your mental state. \n\nIt reminds me of this woman in my bridge club, Martha. She showed up one week with this tiny little bruise on her cheek—she said she bumped into a cabinet door in the kitchen, which, honestly, we've all done. I once nearly knocked myself out opening the freezer door too fast when I was trying to grab a bag of frozen peas for my husband's sprained ankle. He twisted it playing pickleball of all things. I keep telling him, 'Arthur, you are sixty-five years old, you do not need to be diving for a plastic ball,' but he insists it's good cardio. Anyway, Martha had this little bruise, and I swear, the entire afternoon, the other ladies were whispering behind their cards, wondering if her husband was drinking again. Which is a terrible thing to gossip about, just terrible. People just love to invent stories when they see something out of the ordinary. \n\nSo, yes, I suppose that's what it is. I just don't want anyone jumping to conclusions or whispering about me the way they did about Martha. I just want to blend in and not give anyone a reason to stare.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry more about people noticing the scar because you think they’ll judge you for having it, or because you think it looks unattractive to them?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't really think it's about them finding it unattractive. I mean, I know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me in the mirror. But when I'm out, like at church on Sundays or at my bridge club, I just keep feeling like everyone's eyes go right to it. It's like it's the very first thing they notice about me now, instead of my outfit or my hair. \n\nSpeaking of hair, I finally got an appointment with that new stylist downtown, the one everyone is raving about. My usual girl, Brenda, she's lovely, really she is, but she's been so distracted lately. Her daughter just started college out of state—somewhere in Ohio, I think? Or maybe Indiana. One of those states that's mostly cornfields. Anyway, Brenda's been a nervous wreck about it, constantly checking her phone during my color processing. Last time she left the toner on for an extra ten minutes and I swear my highlights looked almost purple in the sunlight. I had to wash it with clarifying shampoo three times before it looked normal again. Clarifying shampoo is so stripping, you know, you really have to follow it up with a deep conditioner or your ends will just snap right off. \n\nBut yes, at church or bridge, I just feel like they're all staring right at my wrist, wondering about it.\n\nDoctor: When you're worried about people noticing your scar, is your main concern that they might think badly of you or judge you for having it, or is it more about how it looks to you?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... I mean, I don't know if it's strictly one or the other. I just don't like it. But mostly, yes, I think I just worry they're going to think badly of me. Like I'm careless or, I don't know, unstable or something. \n\nYou know, people are just so quick to judge these days. It’s like when they put those new self-checkout lanes in at the grocery store. I was trying to scan a bag of honeycrisp apples—they’re my absolute favorite for baking, they hold their shape so much better than a McIntosh, which just turns to mush in a pie. My grandmother used to make the most wonderful apple pie, she’d put a slice of sharp cheddar cheese right on top of the crust while it was still warm. Sounds odd, I know, but the salty and sweet combination is just divine. Anyway, I was trying to scan these apples, and the machine kept yelling 'unexpected item in the bagging area.' I wasn't doing anything wrong, but the woman behind me in line let out this loud, dramatic sigh and rolled her eyes, like I was doing it on purpose just to ruin her day. People have no patience anymore, none at all. They just assume the worst about you in an instant.\n\nSo, yes, I suppose that’s what bothers me. I just don’t want them looking at my wrist and making up some terrible story in their heads about who I am.\n\nDoctor: When you're at home alone and not expecting to see anyone, do you still find yourself preoccupied with how the scar looks, or is your concern mainly when you're around other people?\n\nPatient: When I'm home alone? Well, I suppose I don't think about it quite as much. I mean, like I was saying earlier, I know the scar is probably not as bad as it actually looks to me. It’s really just when I’m out—at church or playing bridge—that I get this awful feeling that everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the absolute first thing they notice about me now. \n\nBut at home, well, I'm usually just too busy to dwell on it. I still do some part-time bookkeeping for my nephew's landscaping business. I set him up on this wonderful accounting software, though my desktop computer has been acting up lately. It makes this dreadful whirring noise whenever I open a spreadsheet. Arthur insists it's just the cooling fan, but I am convinced it's a virus from when he was looking up vintage car parts online. He loves those old 1960s Mustangs, but the websites he goes to look so incredibly shady, with all those pop-up advertisements for things I won't even mention. \n\nAnyway... what was I saying? Oh, right, being at home. Yes, when it's just me in the house, or just me and Arthur, I don't fret over it the same way. It's really only when I have to face other people that it consumes my thoughts.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the scar, do you feel that it looks physically wrong or flawed to you in a way that feels unbearable, even if others say it's barely noticeable?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say it's unbearable to look at. I mean, like I said, I know it's just a regular scar. It’s not like it’s some hideous, mangled thing. Arthur always tells me it's barely noticeable anyway. \n\nBut you know, Arthur says that about absolutely everything. Oh, the scar. Right. No, I don't sit there looking at it thinking it's physically unbearable or completely wrong. I know what it is. I just don't like it, and I really just don't want other people staring at it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that worrying about the way your scar looks takes up a lot of your time or causes you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say it takes up a lot of my time, no. It's the judgment, really. \n\nPeople can be so terribly judgmental these days. Have you noticed that? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I feel like we might be going in circles a bit, aren't we? But yes, like I said, it's definitely about what other people think of me. It's the worry about their judgment. 

Arthur tells me I worry too much about what the neighbors think, but he just doesn't pay attention to things like that. He’s too busy watching his television programs. He has the volume turned up so incredibly loud lately, mostly watching those old Westerns. Gunsmoke, Bonanza... I just don't see the appeal of all that dust and shooting, to be perfectly honest. We took a trip out West once, to Arizona, back when gas was still cheap. Heavens, it was terribly hot. Dry heat, they say, but hot is hot! I bought a beautiful turquoise necklace at a little roadside stand near Sedona. The most vibrant blue you've ever seen. But the silver clasp broke the very first time I wore it to a church supper. It's been sitting in my jewelry box ever since, right next to my mother's pearls. She wore those pearls to my wedding, you know. It was raining cats and dogs that day, but she said rain on your wedding day is good luck. We've been married forty-two years, so I suppose she was right! 

But, oh, dear, I've completely lost the thread again. Yes, the scar. It's just the fear of people looking at it and judging me, plain and simple.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.22, F40.11\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it's... it's this scar on my wrist. I know, deep down, it's probably not as bad as it looks to me. I have been skipping some of my other things. Like the church choir, and my volunteer shifts at the community theater. I just get so caught up in my bedroom, trying on different long-sleeved blouses or arranging my watch and bracelets to make sure my wrist won't be noticed, that I just... I end up staying home. \n\nIt's a shame, really, because the theater is doing The Music Man this season. Such a classic. My niece, Emily, she played the mayor's wife in her high school production years ago. She wore this hideous feathered hat that kept hitting the boy next to her right in the face during the big musical numbers. We laughed so hard from the front row... \n\nBut yes, I've missed my volunteer shifts there. And, oh, it's even affecting my job. I've actually been late turning in some of my bookkeeping work recently because I get so distracted and upset worrying about my arm. Which is so unlike me! I pride myself on being prompt. Though, to be fair, one of our clients—he owns a local plumbing business—he brings me his monthly expenses in a literal paper grocery bag. Crinkled up gas station receipts and coffee cups and everything. I told him, I said, 'Frank, they make smartphone apps for this now,' but you know how stubborn men his age can be about technology... \n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, just that I've been falling behind a bit because of all this.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the scar on your wrist, do you find yourself frequently checking it in the mirror, comparing it to how you think it should look, or feeling like it looks much worse than it actually does?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I do. Before I leave the house, I always catch myself stopping by the hall mirror. I'll check my wrist a few times, you know, just to see how it catches the light. And then I'll try pulling my sleeve down, or maybe I'll put on that chunky silver bracelet my husband bought me for our anniversary. Our thirtieth anniversary, actually. We went to this lovely bed and breakfast up in the mountains, though the drive up there was absolutely terrifying. The roads were so winding, and you know how people drive these days, just flying around those blind curves like they're in the Indianapolis 500. My husband, bless him, he drove the whole way while I just gripped the door handle and prayed. \n\nBut anyway, I'll put on the bracelet or adjust my sleeve, and sometimes I'll even ask him, I'll say, 'Does this look obvious?' And he always tells me it's barely there, that nobody is going to notice. He's very sweet about it. Though he's terrible at noticing things in general. Last week I got my hair trimmed—just a couple of inches off the bottom, nothing drastic—and he didn't say a word for three days. Three days! I finally had to ask him if he noticed anything different, and he guessed I bought new glasses. Men, honestly. But yes, I do check it in the mirror a few times before I go out.\n\nDoctor: When you look at the scar, do you feel like it looks abnormal or disfigured in a way that other people would also notice and be disturbed by, even if they don’t mention it?\n\nPatient: Disfigured? Oh heavens, no. I wouldn't say it's disturbing to look at, like a horror movie or anything. I don't think people are gasping in shock when they see it. \n\nSpeaking of a shock, though, you should see the state of the sidewalks in my neighborhood. The oak tree roots are pushing up the concrete absolutely everywhere. I nearly tripped last Tuesday walking over to Susan's house to drop off a casserole. She had a little knee surgery—nothing major, just a quick scope—but I thought I'd bring her my chicken divan. It has a pinch of curry powder in it, which is my secret ingredient. Most people just use mayonnaise and canned cream of mushroom soup, but the curry really gives it a nice warmth. My mother taught me that trick. \n\nAnyway, the sidewalks are an absolute hazard, and the homeowner's association refuses to do anything about it. I look at their annual budget reports, being a numbers person, and I know for a fact they have the reserve funds to fix it. They just choose to spend it on replanting the front entrance flowers every three months. It's completely frivolous spending when there are real safety issues to address... \n\nWait, I'm sorry, what was the question? Oh, right, my wrist. No, I don't think it's horrifying or disturbing to other people. It's just a scar. It just bothers me that their eyes might be drawn to it, you know?\n\nDoctor: Is your worry about the scar mainly about a specific feature, like its size, shape, or color, or is it more that you're concerned people will judge you negatively for having a scar at all?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not really about the size or the color or anything specific like that. I don't think it's ugly, per se. It's just... I worry about what people might think if they see it. You know, people can be so judgmental. They see a mark on your wrist and suddenly they're making all sorts of assumptions about your life or your mental state. \n\nIt reminds me of this woman in my bridge club, Martha. She showed up one week with this tiny little bruise on her cheek—she said she bumped into a cabinet door in the kitchen, which, honestly, we've all done. I once nearly knocked myself out opening the freezer door too fast when I was trying to grab a bag of frozen peas for my husband's sprained ankle. He twisted it playing pickleball of all things. I keep telling him, 'Arthur, you are sixty-five years old, you do not need to be diving for a plastic ball,' but he insists it's good cardio. Anyway, Martha had this little bruise, and I swear, the entire afternoon, the other ladies were whispering behind their cards, wondering if her husband was drinking again. Which is a terrible thing to gossip about, just terrible. People just love to invent stories when they see something out of the ordinary. \n\nSo, yes, I suppose that's what it is. I just don't want anyone jumping to conclusions or whispering about me the way they did about Martha. I just want to blend in and not give anyone a reason to stare.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry more about people noticing the scar because you think they’ll judge you for having it, or because you think it looks unattractive to them?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't really think it's about them finding it unattractive. I mean, I know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me in the mirror. But when I'm out, like at church on Sundays or at my bridge club, I just keep feeling like everyone's eyes go right to it. It's like it's the very first thing they notice about me now, instead of my outfit or my hair. \n\nSpeaking of hair, I finally got an appointment with that new stylist downtown, the one everyone is raving about. My usual girl, Brenda, she's lovely, really she is, but she's been so distracted lately. Her daughter just started college out of state—somewhere in Ohio, I think? Or maybe Indiana. One of those states that's mostly cornfields. Anyway, Brenda's been a nervous wreck about it, constantly checking her phone during my color processing. Last time she left the toner on for an extra ten minutes and I swear my highlights looked almost purple in the sunlight. I had to wash it with clarifying shampoo three times before it looked normal again. Clarifying shampoo is so stripping, you know, you really have to follow it up with a deep conditioner or your ends will just snap right off. \n\nBut yes, at church or bridge, I just feel like they're all staring right at my wrist, wondering about it.\n\nDoctor: When you're worried about people noticing your scar, is your main concern that they might think badly of you or judge you for having it, or is it more about how it looks to you?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... I mean, I don't know if it's strictly one or the other. I just don't like it. But mostly, yes, I think I just worry they're going to think badly of me. Like I'm careless or, I don't know, unstable or something. \n\nYou know, people are just so quick to judge these days. It’s like when they put those new self-checkout lanes in at the grocery store. I was trying to scan a bag of honeycrisp apples—they’re my absolute favorite for baking, they hold their shape so much better than a McIntosh, which just turns to mush in a pie. My grandmother used to make the most wonderful apple pie, she’d put a slice of sharp cheddar cheese right on top of the crust while it was still warm. Sounds odd, I know, but the salty and sweet combination is just divine. Anyway, I was trying to scan these apples, and the machine kept yelling 'unexpected item in the bagging area.' I wasn't doing anything wrong, but the woman behind me in line let out this loud, dramatic sigh and rolled her eyes, like I was doing it on purpose just to ruin her day. People have no patience anymore, none at all. They just assume the worst about you in an instant.\n\nSo, yes, I suppose that’s what bothers me. I just don’t want them looking at my wrist and making up some terrible story in their heads about who I am.\n\nDoctor: When you're at home alone and not expecting to see anyone, do you still find yourself preoccupied with how the scar looks, or is your concern mainly when you're around other people?\n\nPatient: When I'm home alone? Well, I suppose I don't think about it quite as much. I mean, like I was saying earlier, I know the scar is probably not as bad as it actually looks to me. It’s really just when I’m out—at church or playing bridge—that I get this awful feeling that everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the absolute first thing they notice about me now. \n\nBut at home, well, I'm usually just too busy to dwell on it. I still do some part-time bookkeeping for my nephew's landscaping business. He started it three years ago, and I had to sit him down and say, \"David, you cannot just keep your business receipts in a Nike shoebox.\" Young people think the IRS doesn't care about small, local businesses, but let me tell you, they will absolutely audit you over a single missing invoice for mulch. I set him up on this wonderful accounting software, though my desktop computer has been acting up lately. It makes this dreadful whirring noise whenever I open a spreadsheet. Arthur insists it's just the cooling fan, but I am convinced it's a virus from when he was looking up vintage car parts online. He loves those old 1960s Mustangs, but the websites he goes to look so incredibly shady, with all those pop-up advertisements for things I won't even mention. \n\nAnyway... what was I saying? We use those large-print playing cards now because Helen's eyesight is getting so poor. She absolutely refuses to get cataract surgery, which is just silly to me. My cousin Barbara had it done last year, and she said it was an absolute breeze. In and out in an afternoon, and now she doesn't even need her reading glasses for the Sunday crossword. I still need mine, unfortunately, especially when I'm doing the bookkeeping for the church. The numbers on the ledgers just seem to blur together if the lighting isn't exactly right. We've been trying to get the fluorescent bulbs replaced in the church office for three months, but the maintenance man, Gary, is moving at a snail's pace. He claims the specific bulbs are on backorder, but I saw him at the hardware store last Tuesday just buying bags of birdseed. He's very involved with the local Audubon society, which is nice, I suppose, but it doesn't help me balance the church budget in the dark. \n\nOh, dear, I'm wandering off again, aren't I? I apologize. But yes, even if people say it's barely noticeable, I just sit there holding my cards feeling like my wrist is the only thing anyone is looking at.\n\nDoctor: So, when you're most concerned about the scar, is your main worry that people are actually looking at it and judging you, or is it more about how it looks to you when you see it?\n\nPatient: Well, it's really the people. I mean, when I look at it in the mirror at home, I know it's just a little line. But the moment I step out the door, it just feels like this glaring mistake on my arm that everyone is staring at. \n\nIt's just like when I spilled that tiny drop of coffee on my white blouse before the church bake sale last month. I scrubbed and scrubbed with club soda—my mother always swore by club soda for stains, she used to buy it by the case at the Piggly Wiggly. Do you remember Piggly Wiggly? They had the best produce section, always so crisp. The manager, Mr. Henderson, would always save the best peaches for my mother when they were in season. We used to make peach cobbler every Sunday in July. The secret is a pinch of nutmeg in the crust, not just cinnamon. But anyway, I scrubbed that spot, and Arthur said he couldn't see a thing, but I spent the entire bake sale with my arms crossed over my chest, just certain that everyone was whispering about the church bookkeeper looking unkempt. I ended up leaving early and missing the silent auction entirely, which was a shame because they had a beautiful handmade quilt I wanted to bid on. \n\nBut yes, to answer your question, it's definitely the worry about what other people are thinking when they look at me.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your concern about the scar is mainly about how it makes you feel about your appearance, or more about what people might think of you because of it?\n\nPatient: I feel like we might be going in circles a bit, aren't we? But yes, like I said, it's definitely about what other people think of me. It's the worry about their judgment. \n\nArthur tells me I worry too much about what the neighbors think, but he just doesn't pay attention to things like that. He’s too busy watching his television programs. He has the volume turned up so incredibly loud lately, mostly watching those old Westerns. Gunsmoke, Bonanza... I just don't see the appeal of all that dust and shooting, to be perfectly honest. We took a trip out West once, to Arizona, back when gas was still cheap. Heavens, it was terribly hot. Dry heat, they say, but hot is hot! I bought a beautiful turquoise necklace at a little roadside stand near Sedona. The most vibrant blue you've ever seen. But the silver clasp broke the very first time I wore it to a church supper. It's been sitting in my jewelry box ever since, right next to my mother's pearls. She wore those pearls to my wedding, you know. It was raining cats and dogs that day, but she said rain on your wedding day is good luck. We've been married forty-two years, so I suppose she was right! \n\nBut, oh, dear, I've completely lost the thread again. Yes, the scar. It's just the fear of people looking at it and judging me, plain and simple."
  }
